The author of the 
book of the *History
of the Roman
Popes through Numismatics* <note>original: "Historie der Römischen Päbste per Numism." The study of coins (numismatics) was a popular way in the Baroque era to study history and the legitimacy of rulers.</note>

noted regarding Clement 
VII <note>Pope Clement VII (1478–1534), born Giulio de' Medici. His papacy was marked by the Sack of Rome and the beginning of the English Reformation.</note> that he

struck showing Christ

pillar, scourged with 
rods, and placed 
this inscription:
*After many things, many more remain* <note>original: "Post multa plurima restant." This Latin phrase on the coin refers to the ongoing sufferings of Christ or the church, implying that despite much trial, the greatest weight or significance is yet to come.</note>

# Section.

->I divide this entire little work into three<-
## Main Divisions.

### The First Part
Deals generally with the **Indwelling of God** <note>A central concept in mystical and pietist theology, referring to the belief that God resides spiritually within the soul of the believer.</note>; in which the following is to be noted:
(a) That there is an **Indwelling of God**;
(b) Who that **GOD** is who dwells within us?
(c) Where He dwells?
(d) How He dwells within us?

### The Second Part
->shows:<-
(A) The **disposition** <note>original: "Beschaffenheit." This refers to the inner character, quality, or spiritual state required of a person.</note> of those people in whom **GOD** desires to dwell.
(B) The **Means of Grace** <term>Gnaden-Mittel: Theological term for the instruments—specifically the Word and Sacraments—through which God's grace is believed to be communicated to humanity.</term> consecrated and ordained by **GOD**, through which this **Divine Indwelling** is brought about; these are:
(1.) The **Word of God**.
(2.) Holy **Sacraments** <note>original: "Sacramenta," the Latin plural for the sacred rites of the church.</note>.
(3.) **Faith**.

<meta>catchword: (C) The</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the first two parts of the treatise, focusing on the nature of God's presence within the believer and the specific means—such as scripture and sacraments—that facilitate this union.</summary>
<keywords>Indwelling of God, disposition, Means of Grace, Word of God, Sacraments, Faith, Table of Contents</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the table of contents from the previous page, concluding the list of obstacles to spiritual growth and transitioning to the benefits of the divine presence within the soul.</meta>

(o)
<image-desc>Floral rosettes flank the page number (o) at the top center of the page.</image-desc>

(C) The **obstacles** that destroy this **indwelling of God**, which are:
(1.) **Unbelief**.
(2.) **Prevailing sins**, among these:
(1.) **Drunkenness**.
(2.) **Fornication** and **adultery**.
(3.) **Love of the world** or **worldly pleasure**.
(4.) **Pride** <note>original: "Hoffart," a term frequently used in older German to describe a combination of arrogance, vanity, and haughtiness.</note>.
(5.) **Anger** and **unforgiveness** <note>original: "Unversöhnlichkeit," the inability or refusal to be reconciled with others.</note>.

## The Third Part

Shows the glorious **benefits** <note>original: "Nutzen," referring to the spiritual advantages or fruits of the Christian life.</note> of this **indwelling of God**; such as:
(α) The most lovely **friendship** with **GOD**.
(β) The sweetest **rest** in God.
(γ) **Peace** of conscience.
(δ) The **hearing** of our prayer.
(ε) **Comfort** and **protection** in all tribulations and persecutions.
(ζ) The **perfect, glorious enjoyment** of **GOD** in eternal life.

# Second Main Division.
## Chapter I.
### Contents.

The nature and condition of those in whom God graciously dwells. 1. Not in unregenerate <term>Unregenerate (unwiedergebohren): Referring to those who have not yet experienced spiritual "rebirth" or a personal conversion, a central theme in 17th-century devotional life.</term> Jews and Gentiles; but 2. in justified <term>Justified (gerechtfertigt): A key theological term meaning to be declared righteous in the eyes of God, typically through faith.</term> Christians.

### Detailed Exposition.

--- Page 160 ---

<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>❁ (o) ❁</header>

means <note>original: *tel*, the concluding syllable of *Mittel* (means/instrument) from the previous page.</note> on God’s part, through which God graciously desires to dwell in us; and it is 1. Holy Baptism.

### Elaboration.

Just as we must generally be zealous for all the glory of God, so especially must we be zealous for the honor of His divine institutions <note>original: *Einsetzungen*; refers to the sacraments or ordinances established by Christ.</note>, among which we count Holy Baptism. Therefore, we rightly reject, with a holy zeal, the insolence of the Socinians <note>The Socinians were a rationalist Christian sect that denied the Trinity and viewed sacraments like baptism as mere external ceremonies rather than essential means of grace. To the author, their "insolence" lies in devaluing a divine command.</note>,

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>This page identifies Holy Baptism as the first of the divine "means" through which God dwells within a person. It begins a theological defense of the sacrament, calling for holy zeal against those, like the Socinians, who devalue its spiritual importance.</summary>
<keywords>Holy Baptism, means of grace, Glory of God, divine institutions, Socinians, sacraments, zeal</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the critique of the Socinians from page 120, specifically addressing their views on the sacraments and the dangers of religious indifferentism.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>(0)</header>

that they do not consider the holy sacraments <note>original: *Sacramenta*; the sacred rituals of the church, such as Baptism and Communion, which the author views as essential channels of divine grace.</note> to be means for communicating the grace of God to us; they also believe that errors in the doctrine of the sacraments cannot, in and of themselves, bring harm to one's salvation. ## Chapter XV.

### Summary.

Proves that dominant sins <note>original: "herrschende Sünden." This refers to "besetting" or "reigning" sins—persistent patterns of wrongdoing that a person knowingly allows to rule their life and conscience.</note> are a harmful obstacle: That God does not wish to dwell graciously in one who lives in such things with full intent.

# Chapter XIX.
## Summary.

Reveals the fourth reigning sin <note>original: *herrschende Sünde*; in 17th-century theology, this refers to a particular vice that has gained "dominion" or mastery over a person's will.</note> that hinders the gracious indwelling <note>original: *Einwohnung*; the mystical concept of God living within the believer's soul.</note> of God within us; this is overweening pride <term>Pride: translated from the German *Hoffart*, which implies a haughty, ostentatious, or arrogant disposition.</term>.

## Exposition.

**GOD** is a high **Lord**. High lords of this world enjoy dwelling in magnificent houses, for great birds require great nests; but our high **God** only desires to dwell—
<meta>catchword: —well (completing the word "dwell"; original German "nen" completing "wohnen")</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 19, identifying pride (Hoffart) as the fourth major sin that prevents God from dwelling within the human soul. It begins with a metaphor comparing the grand palaces of worldly lords to the unique requirements of God's chosen dwelling place.</summary>
<keywords>Pride, Arrogance, Indwelling of God, Reigning Sin, Spiritual Life, Metaphor of the Nest</keywords>

# Heart’s Devotion.

O! most righteous God / if You were like a usurer <term>usurer: historically, a person who lends money at interest; here used metaphorically to describe a creditor who demands a pitilessly exact accounting of every debt or sin.</term> / and wished to settle everything strictly with us / then we would already be eternally lost and damned. But Your unspeakable long-suffering <note>original: "Langmuth"; a theological term for God's patient endurance and slowness to anger despite human provocation.</note> is the reason / that we still stand in Your grace. O merciful GOD! grant this grace furthermore: that we [Your gra-] <note>The text breaks off here to be continued on the next page; the catchword "Gna" indicates the completion of the word "Gnade" or "Grace."</note>

<vocab>Heart’s Devotion, Long-suffering, Grace, Usurer, Damned</vocab>

<summary>The author offers a prayer of gratitude, acknowledging that humanity only avoids eternal damnation through God's infinite patience rather than through their own merit. It contrasts the image of a harsh debt-collector with the merciful character of the Divine.</summary>

<keywords>divine mercy, long-suffering, righteousness, grace, spiritual debt, devotion, salvation</keywords>

# Exposition

Just as peace in God flows from the gracious indwelling of God <note>The original term *Einwohnung* refers to the "Indwelling of the Trinity," a mystical belief that God physically and spiritually resides within the believer's soul.</note>, so no less does the joy of conscience. What a noble jewel <note>The German *Kleinod* translates to a precious gem or a highly valued treasure.</note> the joy of conscience is, we can see from the contrast of a troubled and evil conscience. The evil conscience awakens nothing but fear in our

# Chapter VII.
## Contents.

Finally, the last benefit is presented, which we derive from the gracious <term>indwelling of God<note>original: "Einwohnung GOttes." This refers to the "mystical union," a belief that God does not just watch over the believer but actually lives within their soul.</note></term>, and it is the perfect, heartfelt enjoyment of God in eternal life.

## Detailed Elaboration.

I now see the end of my holy undertaking/ and shall finally show the last benefit/ which
we

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the final chapter (Chapter VII), which focuses on the ultimate reward of the believer's journey: the "indwelling of God" and the eternal enjoyment of His presence in the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>eternal life, indwelling of God, spiritual benefit, mystical union, holy undertaking, Baroque theology</keywords>

## Heart's Devotion.

O Lord my God!
do you love me so much
already on earth / that you
graciously dwell within me /
unite me with yourself /
gladden and inwardly satisfy
me / even though I still
always sin; Oh, what will
you then do for me there / <note>The author is contrasting the "here" of earthly life with the "there" of heaven.</note>

<meta>catchword: when (wenn)</meta>

<summary>This page completes a quote from the medieval mystic Thomas à Kempis regarding the soul's desire to be with Christ and begins a "Heart's Devotion" reflecting on God's grace. The text marvels that God would dwell within a person who still sins and anticipates the even greater wonders of the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>Bridegroom, Jesus Christ, Thomas à Kempis, Imitation of Christ, devotion, indwelling of God, grace, spiritual union, afterlife</keywords>
